Full text

Abstract

De-identified data, analysis scripts, figuregeneration code, and redacted AI-coding outputs are available on the Open Science Framework (https://osf.io/6z3xe ) . Raw open-text responses are not publicly shared to minimize risk of re-identification.

Motivational Framing Shapes Effort Judgments in Individuals with Depressive Symptoms

Depression remains a major public health concern worldwide, and anhedonia, the reduced enjoyment of rewarding activities, often persists despite increased health-care expenditures and treatment access (Murray et al., 2015; Ormel et al., 2022). Interventions that increase engagement in rewarding activities, such as behavioral activation, are effective and scalable (Ekers et al., 2014), yet their mechanisms remain incompletely understood (Hird et al., 2024). A central challenge is that depression involves not only reduced reward responsiveness, but also reduced willingness to initiate and sustain effortful action. Individuals with depressive symptoms may overestimate the effort required to initiate or complete personally meaningful activities (Cléry-Melin et al., 2011), and are less willing to mobilize effort for rewards than healthy or remitted peers (Halahakoon et al., 2020; Treadway et al., 2012).

Moreover, computational models have linked depression to heightened effort sensitivity that is partly distinct from reward responsiveness (Berwian et al., 2020; Vinckier et al., 2022) and may normalize during treatment (Norbury et al., 2024). If ordinary activities such as commuting, cooking, studying, or cleaning feel excessively effortful, they may be avoided or abandoned, even when they are objectively important, manageable, and potentially rewarding. Thus, judgments of perceived effort may be a clinically relevant target for understanding and treating motivational dysfunction in depression.

We define effort as the mobilization of resources to execute an action, and perceived effort as a subjective judgment about the experience of mobilizing resources to execute an action (Halperin & Vigotsky, 2024; Hoch et al., 2023; Richter et al., 2016; Shaffer et al., 2022).

Importantly, perceived effort is not a direct readout of effort (Bijleveld, 2018; Wolpe et al., 2024) but rather reflects a metacognitive estimate of the resources required to perform an action. While an energy-conservation principle would dictate that perceived effort is inherently aversive, this is not necessarily the case. The "effort paradox" refers to the observation that effort can be experienced both as a cost to be avoided and as a source of value, meaning, and motivation (Inzlicht et al., 2025; Inzlicht et al., 2018). Effort may deter action when it signals strain, fatigue, or opportunity cost, yet it can also enhance the valuation of task outcomes when it signals commitment or progress toward personally meaningful goals (also see Cheval et al., 2025). A promising account locates this difference in people's motivational attribution of effort. Grund et al. (2024) distinguish effort-by-need-frustration, in which effort reflects negative affective demand, external pressure, and frustrated needs, from effort-by-allocation, in which effort is construed as self-initiated investment of resources toward a goal. Overall, motivational framings of effort may change the interpretive context through which effort becomes costly or valuable (Baars et al., 2020; Koriat et al., 2014).

This raises a more basic question: what is the psychological basis of effort judgments? Subjective effort ratings are not self-contained readouts of the allocation of physical or mental resources. Rather, like all self-relevant judgments, they require a reference frame (Morina, 2021; Vlaev et al., 2011). A rating of 7 out of 10 is meaningful only relative to some implicit or explicit standard: past experiences, other people, expectations or criteria, imagined alternatives, or other standards to gauge the perceived effort associated with the activity. The impact of reference frames on effort perception is well documented in exercise and sport contexts. Perceived effort changes when people compare current exertion to different prior anchors, when tasks are framed as ending easily or with difficulty, when expectations are experimentally manipulated, or when individuals draw on different histories of training and fitness (Hutchinson et al., 2023; Lichti et al., 2023; Malleron et al., 2023; Mothes et al., 2017; Travlos & Marisi, 1996). These findings imply that maladaptive effort perception in depression may not only reflect altered interoceptive or reward-cost processing, but also biased reference frames for interpreting everyday action.

Comparison standards may therefore form the hidden architecture of perceived effort.

Following historical comparison theories (e.g., Albert, 1977; Festinger, 1954; review: Morina, 2021) and prior work on comparison standards in self-relevant judgments (Meyer et al., 2024; Morina et al., 2022), people may judge effort relative to other people (e.g., "others would find this easier"; social comparison), their own past (e.g., "this used to be easier"; temporal comparison), criteria or expectations (e.g., "this should be easy"; criteria-based comparison), or imagined alternatives (e.g., "if circumstances had unfolded differently, this would be easier"; counterfactual comparison). Such standards may not only alter effort estimates, but also whether effort is construed as failure, burden, progress, resilience, or meaningful investment.

This may be particularly relevant in depression, where self-relevant judgments are often shaped by aversive, self-critical comparisons against subjectively superior or unattainable standards (Morina & Schlechter, 2023; Schlechter & Morina, 2024). In the context of effort, such comparisons may transform ordinary activities into evidence of inadequacy: "While I struggle, others manage this without difficulty," "I used to cope better,"

or "I should not find this so hard". Conversely, other standards may normalize effort, highlight prior capability, or support continued engagement. Identifying effort-related comparison standards may therefore clarify how effort becomes psychologically and motivationally meaningful. Here, we studied individuals with depressive symptoms because this group often experiences everyday activities as highly effortful, making high-effort episodes frequent enough to sample and consequential enough to matter clinically. However, the psychological processes examined here are assumed to be general and to operate across clinical and non-clinical populations. Therefore, depression symptom severity is treated here as a continuous source of variation within the sample.

A first way to organize effort-related comparisons is by comparison direction. In this study, we refer to upward comparisons as comparison to lower-effort standards, such that the current activity feels more effortful than it does for others, than it did in the past, than it should, or than it would under better circumstances. Downward comparisons refer to highereffort standards, such that the activity feels comparably less effortful. We treated this distinction as an initial motivational heuristic: upward comparisons may highlight aversive discrepancies, whereas downward comparisons may highlight relative ease or coping.

However, comparison direction does not determine motivational meaning by itself. Upward comparisons can, under some conditions, inspire motivation, whereas downward comparisons may provide reassurance without necessarily promoting change (Buunk et al., 2020; Morina, 2021; Unkelbach et al., 2023). Comparison direction therefore formed the basis of our primary preregistered prediction, which is stated in full in the Present Study section. Indeed, some evidence indicates that the type of comparison is just as important as comparison direction. That is, self-relevant judgments can draw on multiple standard types, and prior work suggests that the standards people use vary across domains: social standards may be especially salient for appearance-related judgments, social and criteria-based standards for academic self-evaluations, and temporal-past or criteria-based standards for well-being judgments (e.g., McCarthy et al., 2023; Meyer et al., 2023; Morina et al., 2022). Yet, to our knowledge, no study has systematically examined multiple comparison standards that people recruit when judging everyday effort. This is important because effort may plausibly be judged against several different benchmarks: other people's apparent ease, one's own previous functioning, expectations about how hard the activity should be, or imagined alternative circumstances. Whether framing effects depend on standard type was therefore tested as a separate, non-directional hypothesis.

Present Study

We tested whether motivational framing changes the comparison standards individuals with depressive symptoms identify as applicable to their everyday effort judgments. We focused on everyday activities because effort-based difficulties in depression often unfold outside the laboratory, in ordinary actions, e.g., including social interaction or self-care. Laboratory effort tasks are indispensable for isolating mechanisms of effort-based decision making (Chong & Bonnelle, 2016), yet they are less suited to capturing the reference frames through which people interpret effort in daily life, where activities are embedded in personal histories, expectations, social roles, and imagined alternatives (cf. Culbreth et al., 2020). We therefore combined an adapted Day Reconstruction Method (Kahneman et al., 2004) with a structured assessment of comparison standards. This procedure was designed to make potentially relevant standards reportable in retrospect. Importantly, this approach does not attempt to establish which standards were causally active when perceived effort was initially rated, but to map the type and relevance of comparison standards that may form a reference frame for their effort judgments.

Participants with at least mild depressive symptoms completed two counterbalanced sessions. In one session, effort was framed as allocation, that is, as self-initiated investment in an activity or goal. In the other session, effort was framed as need frustration, that is, as affective demand, burden, fatigue, or frustration. For the two activities identified as most effortful during each session, participants completed a comparison-standard assessment that crossed four standard types (social, temporal-past, criteria-based, and counterfactual) with two comparison directions (upward and downward). For each standard, we assessed endorsement (yes/no), intensity (i.e., how strongly participants believe the standard had informed their effort rating), and perceived discrepancy to the respective standard. Within this design, endorsement reflects the availability or applicability of a candidate reference standard after the effort judgment, whereas intensity provides an estimate of how strongly participants believed this standard had informed the earlier rating. To distinguish framing effects on effort from more general judgments of task difficulty, participants also rated tasklevel complexity (e.g., number of steps, decisions, or physical demands). Finally, to examine prospective motivational implications, participants rated how effortful it would be to repeat the activity (prospective effort) and how confident they were that they could do so (prospective self-efficacy).

We preregistered the directional prediction that framing effort as need frustration would make motivationally deleterious reference frames more salient. Specifically, we expected need-frustration framing to increase endorsement of upward comparison standards relative to allocation framing, whereas endorsement of downward standards was not expected to differ between framings. This pattern corresponds to an Instruction Type × Comparison Direction interaction (H1a). The same directional pattern was predicted for comparison intensity (H1b). Within upward comparisons, we further expected needfrustration framing to increase perceived discrepancy to the standards (H1c). Because upward, high-discrepancy standards were assumed to be especially threatening for future action, we also predicted lower prospective self-efficacy under need-frustration than allocation framing (H2) 1 . Since effort-related reference frames have not been systematically mapped, we also preregistered a non-directional hypothesis that instruction effects could depend on comparison-standard type, yielding Instruction Type × Comparison Standard or Instruction 1 In the OSF preregistration, we erroneously formulated this expected effect in the reverse direction (higher prospective self-efficacy under need frustration). Here, we state the intended direction.

Type × Comparison Direction × Comparison Standard interactions (H3).

Finally, we explored whether instructional framing changed perceived effort (H4) and prospective effort (H5), tentatively expecting lower effort under allocation than under need frustration.

Transparency and Openness

The study hypotheses, design, and primary analysis plan were preregistered on OSF (https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/6N4HR). Study materials, including verbatim German effort-framing instructions, effort scale, and comparison-standard prompts, are provided in the Supplemental Materials. De-identified data, analysis scripts, figure-generation code, and output are available on OSF (https://osf.io/6z3xe) with the exception of raw open-text answers to prevent de-anonymization. We report how we determined our sample size, all data exclusions, all manipulations, and all measures in the study. Deviations from the preregistered plan and exploratory analyses are identified as such in the manuscript and in Supplemental Appendix E.

The authors used ChatGPT (GPT-5.5; OpenAI, 2026) and Claude-Opus-5.5 (Anthropic, 2026) as assistive tools during manuscript preparation, including writing, organizing, and auditing analysis scripts, drafting and editing text. Analysis scripts were written with substantial assistance from these large language models, primarily Claude-Opus-5.5, which were also used to audit the scripts, check consistency between analysis output and manuscript text. All analytic decisions were made by the authors; all scripts were run by the first author, and their output was checked against the reported results. A locally hosted model, gptoss-120b (OpenAI, 2025), was used for AI-assisted coding of open-text responses (Supplemental Material). The authors take full responsibility for all analyses, interpretations, and content.

Method Participants

Fifty-one participants (40 women, 10 men, 1 diverse) completed the study. Mean age was 24.1 years (SD = 4.7). Of these, 46 (90.2%) were students. Participants were recruited via advertisements and social media and first completed an online screening. Inclusion criteria were age between 18 and 65 years, native-level German proficiency, and at least mild depressive symptoms, defined as a score ≥ 5 on the Patient Health Questionnaire (PHQ-8; Sample size was determined a priori, prior to data collection, using a simulation-based power analysis with the R package Superpower (Lakens & Caldwell, 2021); this analysis was not included in the preregistration. We simulated a 2 (Instruction Type) × 2 (Comparison Direction) within-subjects design with N = 50, assuming cell means that differed by 0.3 SD in a crossover pattern (upward comparisons higher under need frustration, downward comparisons higher under allocation), SD = 1, and a correlation of r = .50 between repeated measures. Across 1,000 simulations (α = .05), power to detect the Instruction Type × Comparison Direction interaction was 84.3% (partial η² = .16). This analysis served as an approximation of the primary hypotheses (H1a, H1b) with a repeated-measures ANOVA on a continuous outcome. The power-analysis script is available on OSF. The sampling target of N = 50 was exceeded by one participant.

Experimental design

The study used a within-subjects crossover design with Instruction Type (allocation vs. need frustration) as the manipulated factor. Each participant completed two sessions one week apart, one under each framing, with framing order counterbalanced across participants (25 allocation first, 26 need frustration first). In each session, participants selected the two reconstructed episodes they had rated as most effortful. For each of these episodes, they completed a comparison-standard assessment crossing four Comparison Standard Types with two Comparison Directions. This yields 2 framings × 2 episodes × 4 standard types × 2 directions = 32 standard judgments per participant, or 1,632 judgments in total. "Episode" denotes which of the two selected episodes within a session was assessed. It was included as a fixed effect to account for the nested data structure and was not of theoretical interest.

Procedure

After online screening and informed consent, eligible participants completed two study sessions, 7 days apart, both sessions taking place at the same time of day. In both sessions, participants reconstructed everyday activities from the previous day, rated the effort associated with these activities under the assigned instructional framing, and subsequently completed the reference-frame assessment for the two activities they had rated as most effortful. Day reconstruction and effort ratings were completed on paper. The subsequent reference frame assessment for the selected episodes was completed via Unipark on a laboratory computer.

Day Reconstruction and Effort Ratings

Everyday activities were assessed using an adapted version of the Day Reconstruction Method (DRM; Kahneman et al., 2004; Meyer et al., 2012) that consisted of two phases. In the first phase, participants reconstructed the previous day by listing all distinct activity episodes they could remember. They received structured reconstruction sheets with space for up to 30 episodes. For each episode, participants wrote down a short label describing their main activity. They were also given space for personal notes that were solely used to facilitate recall; they were neither collected nor analyzed.

In the second phase, participants received one of the two effort-framing instructions (see next section). They then indicated the start and end time of each reconstructed episode and rated the effort associated with the main activity of that episode. Effort was rated on a Borg-style, verbally anchored 10-point scale developed for this study (1 = no effort at all;10 = impossibly high effort).

Effort-Framing Manipulation

The manipulation varied the manner in which participants were asked to construe "effort" before they were asked to rate the activities. In the allocation condition, effort was defined as the amount participants voluntarily invested in a task or activity. The instruction emphasized effort as self-initiated investment directed toward performing an activity or attaining a goal, often reflecting the personal importance, meaningfulness, or goal relevance of the activity. In the need-frustration condition, effort was defined as the extent to which a task or activity was emotionally demanding. The instruction emphasized effort as reflecting negative affective demands imposed by the activity, which is often experienced as burdensome, obstructive, tiring, or frustrating. The full German wording of the effort-framing instructions and effort scale is provided in the Supplemental Material.

Reference Frame Assessment

After rating all reconstructed activities, participants selected the two episodes with the highest effort ratings. For each selected episode, they first provided an open-text explanation of why they had chosen the respective effort value. They then completed a structured reference-frame assessment for each episode, which crossed the four types of comparison standards (social, temporal-past, criteria-based, counterfactual) with two comparison directions (upward, downward). The order of the standard types was randomized for each participant, as was the order of upward and downward directions within each standard.

Each comparison cell followed the same structure. First, participants were reminded of the activity label and the corresponding effort value they had provided. They were then asked whether a given standard applied. For example, the upward social prompt asked, "Are there people in your environment who, in your view, would associate the same task or activity with less effort?", and the upward criteria-based prompt asked, "Do you think that you actually should associate the same task or activity with lower effort?" Downward prompts referred to more effort accordingly. All prompts are listed in Table S2. This yes/no response served as the binary endorsement measure. If participants endorsed the comparison standard, they provided a brief open-text description of the standard. They then rated how strongly they had thought about this standard when making their original effort rating, using a scale from1 = very little to7 = very strongly. Finally, they rated the perceived discrepancy between their current effort experience and the comparison standard, using a 7-point scale anchored at1 = the same and7 = much greater (lower) for upward (downward) comparisons.

Episode-level effort-related measures

Task Complexity

Following each reference frame assessment, participants also rated the complexity of the episode's activity. Complexity was introduced as a task characteristic that may reflect, for example, the number of steps or decisions required to perform the activity, or the degree to which the activity was physically demanding (cf. Grund et al., 2024). Participants rated how complex the activity had been for them on a 10-point scale analogous to the effort scale, ranging from1 = no complexity at all / not demanding at all to10 = impossible complexity / too demanding to perform.

Prospective Effort

For each activity, participants were asked to imagine performing the same activity again the following day and to rate how much effort they would associate with doing so.

Prospective effort was rated on the same 10-point effort scale as perceived effort, with higher values indicating greater anticipated effort.

Prospective Self-Efficacy

Participants rated how confident they were that they could perform the same activity again the following day. Responses were given on a 7-point scale ranging from1 = very little confidence to7 = very strong confidence.

Depressive Symptoms, Anhedonia, and Health-Related Well-Being

Depressive Symptoms

Depressive symptoms were assessed during screening with the German 8-item version of the Patient Health Questionnaire (PHQ-8; Kroenke et al., 2009). The PHQ-8 assesses depressive symptoms over the past two weeks on eight 4-point items ranging from 0 (not at all) to 3 (nearly every day). A sum score of 5 or higher, corresponding to at least mild depressive symptoms, was required for inclusion. Internal consistency in the current sample was acceptable, α = .744.

Reward responsiveness / Anhedonia

Reward responsiveness / anhedonia was assessed directly after screening using items from the extended German translation (Blackwell et al., 2018) of the Dimensional Anhedonia Rating Scale (DARS; Rizvi et al., 2015). We used the 17 items from the original scale, which cover 4 domains of pleasant activities. Items assess motivation, willingness to exert effort, and pleasure associated with these activity domains (e.g., "I would make an effort to spend time having these experiences") on 5-point scales ranging from 0 (not at all) to 4 (very much). Sum scores can range from 0 to 68, with higher scores indicating greater reward responsiveness and thus lower levels of anhedonia. Internal consistency was good, α = .889.

Health-Related Well-Being

Health-related well-being was assessed after screening with the adapted German SF-12-ENSURE version of the 12-item Short-Form Health Survey (Richter et al., 2024). The instrument assesses physical and mental health-related well-being using twelve items with varying 5-point response formats, with some items allowing "not applicable" responses that were treated as missing. Negatively poled items were reverse-coded such that higher values indicated better health-related well-being. Following the published scoring instructions, unweighted mean scores were calculated for Physical Health (PH; α = .747) and Mental Health (MH; α = .838).

Statistical Analysis

Analyses were conducted in R/RStudio (Posit Team, 2026; R Core Team, 2026) For one judgment, the participant noted in the open-text field that an initial 'yes' had been a misclick; its residual follow-up ratings were set to missing. Following this correction, 810 of the 1,632 standard judgments (49.6%) were endorsements: 507 of 816 upward judgments (62.1%) and 303 of 816 downward judgments (37.1%). Intensity and discrepancy ratings were available for all 810 endorsed standards. For binary endorsement data, the proportion of "no" responses was inspected to evaluate potential zero inflation. The observed proportion of non-endorsement did not exceed the expected proportion under a standard binomial model by more than 10%. Thus, binary comparison endorsement was analyzed using mixed-effects logistic regression with the glmer function from the lme4 package. Intensity ratings were analyzed using linear mixed-effects models with the lmer function. Discrepancy ratings were analyzed using the same mixed-effects modeling approach, with particular emphasis on upward comparisons, as specified in the hypotheses. The linear mixed models were estimated with REML. As preregistered, non-significant interaction terms not pertaining to directional hypotheses were removed stepwise (highest p first, respecting hierarchy).

Exploratory analyses examined perceived effort and prospective effort as outcomes, comparing the two instructional framing conditions. Alpha was set to .05, two-sided, for all analyses. Pairwise instruction contrasts were additionally adjusted using the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure within each family of follow-up contrasts. Full model outputs and pairwise contrasts are reported in the Supplemental Material.

Results

Sample characteristics

At prescreening, participants reported moderate depressive symptoms on average, as indicated by PHQ-8 scores (M = 10.2, SD = 4.0, range = 5-19; i.e., mild to moderately severe).

Reward responsiveness / anhedonia, assessed with the DARS, had a mean score of M = 46.8 (SD = 10.2, range = 22-63) on the 0-68 scale. SF-12-ENSURE scores indicated relatively preserved physical health-related well-being amid slightly lowered mental health-related wellbeing (PH: M = 4.18, SD = 0.59; MH: M = 3.08, SD = 0.67). The activities nominated as most effortful by participants spanned a broad range of everyday domains, most commonly workor study-related tasks (e.g., exam preparation, writing a thesis), social interactions (e.g., phone calls, meeting friends), and self-care or health-related activities (e.g., getting up, showering, therapy sessions), but also household chores, commuting, errands, and physical activity.

Comparison standard endorsement

The mixed-effects logistic regression 2 indicated a significant three-way Instruction Type × Comparison Direction × Comparison Standard Type interaction, χ²(3) = 8.29, p = .040; a likelihood-ratio test confirmed this interaction, χ²(3) = 11.79, p = .008. Because the three-way interaction was significant, no terms were removed under the preregistered model-reduction rule. The hypothesized Instruction Type × Comparison Direction interaction was not significant, χ²(1) = 1.33, p = .249.

Following up the three-way interaction, additional mixed-effects logistic regression models were fitted per comparison direction. For upward comparisons, there was a significant Instruction Type × Comparison Standard Type interaction, χ²(3) = 13.65, p = .003. Needfrustration instruction increased the likelihood of endorsing upward criteria-based comparisons (estimated probability = .663) relative to the allocation instruction (.435), OR = 2.55, SE = 0.77, z = 3.09, p = .002. The opposite pattern emerged for upward temporal-past 2 The initially fitted model produced a convergence warning; therefore, the model and follow-up analyses were refitted using the bobyqa optimizer with a higher iteration limit. All refitted models converged successfully and were not singular, while the interpretation of findings remained virtually unchanged. Hence, results from the refitted models are reported. comparisons (need frustration: .490; allocation: .642), OR = 0.54, SE = 0.16, z = -2.08, p = .038.

No instructional-framing difference emerged for upward counterfactual comparisons, OR = 1.31, SE = 0.39, z = 0.90, p = .366, or upward social comparisons, OR = 1.00, SE = 0.35, z = 0.00, p > .999.

For downward comparisons, the Instruction Type × Comparison Standard Type interaction was also significant, χ²(3) = 8.54, p = .036. Need-frustration framing increased the likelihood of endorsing downward counterfactual comparisons (.533) relative to the allocation instruction (.324), OR = 2.38, SE = 0.75, z = 2.76, p = .006. No significant instructional-framing differences emerged for downward criteria-based comparisons, OR = 0.15, SE = 0.17, z = -1.71, p = .087, downward social comparisons, OR = 1.10, SE = 0.34, z = 0.31, p = .758, or downward temporal-past comparisons, OR = 1.00, SE = 0.31, z = 0.00, p > .999. Downward criteria-based comparisons were rarely endorsed (1 and 6 endorsements of 102 judgments, respectively), rendering this contrast imprecise.

After BH correction across the eight contrasts above, the upward criteria-based (p BH = .016) and downward counterfactual effects (p BH = .023) remained significant, whereas the upward temporal-past effect did not (p BH = .101). Thus, the predicted general Instruction Type

Comparison intensity

Comparison intensity (n = 810) was analyzed with the same model structure. In the full model, intensity differed by Standard Type, χ²(3) = 58.52, p < .001, and Direction × Standard Type, χ²(3) = 12.89, p = .005, but instruction effects depended neither on Standard Type, χ²(3) = 5.06, p = .168, nor on Direction and Standard Type jointly, χ²(3) = 4.81, p = .186. After removing these interactions as preregistered, the predicted Instruction Type × Comparison Direction interaction was not significant, χ²(1) = 0.01, p = .938. Thus, neither H1b nor H3 was supported for intensity. The reduced model showed an unexpected main effect of Instruction Type (need frustration: M = 3.15, SE = 0.17; allocation: M = 2.83, SE = 0.16), χ²(1) = 8.71, p = .003, which was not significant in the full model, χ²(1) = 0.09, p = .761, or after excluding the sparse downward criteria-based cells, χ²(1) = 0.99, p = .320 (Figure S1).

Because intensity ratings were available only for endorsed standards, we conducted an exploratory analysis combining endorsement and intensity into a relevance score, with non-endorsed standards coded as 0 and endorsed standards coded with their intensity rating.

This analysis yielded a significant Instruction Type × Direction × Standard Type interaction and confirmed that upward criteria-based standards were more relevant under need-frustration than allocation framing. Full results are reported in the Supplemental Material.

Comparison discrepancy

Upward discrepancy ratings (n = 507) were analyzed with Instruction Type, Standard Type, their interaction, and Episode. The Instruction Type × Standard Type interaction was not significant, χ²(3) = 1.53, p = .676, and was removed as preregistered. There was a significant effect of Instruction Type, χ²(1) = 4.78, p = .029. Contrary to expectations, discrepancy ratings were higher under allocation (M = 4.80, SE = 0.16) than under need-frustration instructions (M = 4.54, SE = 0.15). There also was a significant effect of Comparison Standard, χ²(3) = 33.15, p < .001. Marginal means indicated that upward discrepancies were higher for social (M = 5.04, SE = 0.17) and temporal standards (M = 4.97, SE = 0.18) than for criteria-based (M = 4.36, SE = 0.18) and counterfactual standards (M = 4.31, SE = 0.18). Model-estimated upward discrepancy ratings by instruction and standard type are illustrated in the Supplemental Material.

Perceived and anticipated effort, complexity, and self-efficacy

Linear mixed-effects models examined episode-level ratings of perceived effort, complexity, prospective effort, and prospective self-efficacy as a function of Instruction Type, controlling for Episode and including a random intercept for participant ID. Instruction Type significantly modulated perceived effort: Effort ratings were higher in the allocation condition (M = 7.04, SE = 0.16) than in the need-frustration condition (M = 6.55, SE = 0.16), t(151) = 3.17, p = .002. Instruction Type also significantly predicted prospective effort, again with higher ratings in the allocation condition (M = 6.19, SE = 0.20) than in the need frustration condition (M = 5.50, SE = 0.20), t(151) = 3.29, p = .001. Complexity did not differ significantly between conditions, t(151) = 1.87, p = .063. Prospective self-efficacy did not differ between instructional framings, t(151) = 0.04, p = .965.

Thus, the exploratory prediction that effort and prospective effort might be lower under allocation than need frustration was not supported; the observed pattern was in the opposite direction. The predicted effect on prospective self-efficacy was also not supported.

Table 1 summarizes the key model results.

Exploratory analyses

Exploratory moderation of effort by depressive symptoms and anhedonia. Given the unexpected finding that perceived effort was higher in the allocation than in the needfrustration condition, we conducted exploratory post hoc analyses to test whether this effect depended on individual differences in depressive symptoms and anhedonia. Specifically, we fitted linear mixed-effects models predicting perceived effort from Instruction Type, either PHQ-8 depressive symptoms or anhedonia, and their interaction, controlling for Episode and including a random intercept for participant. Parallel models were fitted for complexity to examine whether any moderation effects were specific to effort rather than reflecting general perceived task difficulty.

For PHQ-8, there was a significant Instruction Type × depressive symptoms interaction for perceived effort, χ²(1) = 6.01, p = .014. Simple-slope analyses indicated that higher depressive symptoms were associated with greater perceived effort in the need frustration condition, slope = 0.44, SE = 0.15, 95% CI [0.14, 0.75], but not in the allocation condition, slope = 0.07, SE = 0.15, 95% CI [-0.24, 0.37], with slopes differing significantly, p = .015. The corresponding interaction was not significant for complexity, χ²(1) = 1.85, p = .174. Similarly, anhedonia significantly moderated the effect of Instruction Type on perceived effort, χ²(1) = 12.31, p < .001. The association between anhedonia and perceived effort was more positive in the need frustration condition, slope = 0.27, SE = 0.16, 95% CI [-0.04, 0.59], and more negative in the allocation condition, slope = -0.25, SE = 0.16, 95% CI [-0.57, 0.06], with the slopes differing significantly, p < .001. Again, the analogous interaction was not significant for complexity, χ²(1) = 1.09, p = .297. These exploratory findings suggest that symptom severity and anhedonia were selectively associated with perceived effort differently across framings, whereas no such moderation emerged for complexity. Figure 2 illustrates the moderation of perceived effort by depressive symptoms and anhedonia across instructional framings. Note. Lines show model-estimated perceived effort as a function of standardized depressive symptoms (left panel) and standardized anhedonia (right panel), separately by instructional framing. Points show participant-level condition means. Higher anhedonia scores reflect lower DARS reward responsiveness. Shaded ribbons indicate 95% confidence intervals.

Order effects. We refitted the models with Session as an additional fixed effect and tested the Instruction Type × Session interaction, which indicates differential carryover in an AB/BA crossover. Endorsement differed between sessions, χ²(1) = 9.16, p = .002, but the threeway interaction remained significant, χ²(3) = 8.50, p = .037, and there was no overall carryover, χ²(1) = 2.74, p = .098. The upward criteria-based and downward counterfactual effects showed no carryover, χ²(1) = 0.02, p = .888, and χ²(1) = 1.97, p = .161, and were present in first-session data alone (OR = 3.48, p = .049; OR = 4.62, p = .007). In contrast, the upward temporal-past effect showed carryover, χ²(1) = 5.31, p = .021, and was absent in first-session data (OR = 1.17, p = .728). Instruction effects on intensity, upward discrepancy, and perceived and prospective effort were unchanged when controlling for Session; the effort effects showed no carryover (ps ≥ .188) and were present in first-session data (ps ≤ .028).

Open text analyses

The open-text data were analyzed to serve two auxiliary purposes: (a) as a manipulation check on how participants construed effort under each framing (i.e., as allocation vs. as need frustration), and (b) as a validity check on whether the described standards matched the intended standard type and direction. They do not constitute an independent test of the hypotheses.

Manipulation check.

As a post-hoc manipulation/content check, open-text explanations of effort ratings were coded for need-frustration and allocation content using gpt-oss-120b as a locally hosted LLM (see Supplemental Material for human-LLM agreement).

Coding was conducted row-wise and blind to instruction condition. Each explanation was rated from 0 to 3 for need-frustration content and allocation content, and a dominant attribution category was assigned. All 204 open-text explanations were successfully coded.

Scores were averaged within participant and instruction condition. Need-frustration content was higher in the need-frustration condition (M = 1.59, SD = 0.72) than in the allocation condition (M = 0.77, SD = 0.70), paired-samples t(50) = 6.76, p < .001, dz = 0.95. Conversely, allocation content was higher in the allocation condition (M = 1.62, SD = 0.95) than in the need-frustration condition (M = 0.52, SD = 0.65), paired-samples t(50) = -8.04, p < .001, dz = -1.13. Descriptively, dominant-attribution codes showed the same pattern: explanations in the need-frustration condition were more often classified as need-frustration dominant (72.5%) than explanations in the allocation condition (23.5%), whereas explanations in the allocation condition were more often classified as allocation dominant (55.9%) than explanations in the need-frustration condition (14.7%). Thus, the manipulation seems to have shifted the semantic construal of effort in the intended direction.

Validity check and sensitivity analyses.

As an AI-assisted validity check, endorsed standard descriptions were coded for obvious mismatches with the intended standard type or direction. Of 810 standard descriptions, 58 (7.2%; one mismatched both type and direction) were identified as mismatches.

Standard-type fit was high: 767 descriptions (94.7%) matched the intended standard type, 7 (0.9%) were unclear, and 36 (4.4%) were type mismatches.

Direction was often under-specified, as expected for brief responses: 473 descriptions (58.4%) matched the intended direction, 314 (38.8%) were unclear, and 23 (2.8%) contradicted the intended direction. After recoding mismatches as non-endorsed, the effect on criteria-upward endorsement was preserved, OR = 3.38, SE = 1.17, z = 3.53, p < .001, as was the effect on downward counterfactual endorsement, OR = 2.23, SE = 0.78, z = 2.29, p = .022. The temporalupward effect remained descriptively in the same direction but was no longer significant, OR = 0.65, SE = 0.19, z = -1.45, p = .148. This suggests that the main pattern of findings was not driven by misunderstandings of the comparison prompts, although the temporal-past effect should be interpreted more cautiously. Since human raters coded fewer mismatches than the LLM (Supplemental Material), this sensitivity analysis is, if anything, stringent.

Illustrative content of focal standards.

To illustrate the content of the three focal comparison standards that differed by instruction, Table 2 presents hand-selected examples from the cleaned standard descriptions. These examples are intended to make the quantitative effects more concrete, not to establish exhaustive qualitative categories or definitive motivational meanings. A fuller overview with additional examples is provided in Appendix Table S1. Discussion

The present study examined whether experimentally framing effort as need-frustrating versus self-initiated allocation changes the comparison standards people with depressive symptoms identify as relevant to everyday effort judgments. In general, upward standards were endorsed considerably more often than downward standards, consistent with the selection of each session's most effortful activities, for which lower-effort benchmarks are more readily available than higher-effort ones. Endorsement was nonetheless far from ceiling, indicating that participants did not simply accept every proposed standard. Motivational framing did not simply increase upward over downward comparisons, as predicted. Instead, it selectively altered the type of comparison standards participants endorsed: Needfrustration framing increased upward criteria-based and downward counterfactual standards.

Thus, our first major finding was that motivational framing changed which comparison standards participants identified as applicable to their everyday effort judgments, but not in a way reducible to comparison direction alone.

This finding warrants an important methodological qualification. Because endorsement of comparison standards was assessed only after participants had rated perceived effort, their endorsement does not provide evidence that these standards causally shaped the original effort ratings. Rather, endorsement reflects which standards participants retrospectively identified as applicable during our structured reference-frame assessment.

The subsequent intensity item asked participants how strongly they had thought about each standard when making the earlier effort rating, but the primary intensity model did not show the predicted instruction effects. Accordingly, the strongest evidence concerns framingrelated changes in the availability or endorsement of candidate reference standards, whereas evidence for changes in retrospectively perceived influence is weaker. The relevance score distinguishes standards that merely apply from standards that participants report having actively considered. If comparison standards shape the motivational meaning of effort, their impact should depend on how much a standard was considered, not only on whether it could be endorsed. The relevance score therefore provides a closer, although exploratory and still retrospective, approximation of a standard's role in the judgment than endorsement alone.

Only the upward criteria-based effect survived this stricter criterion. Nonetheless, the findings identify comparison standards that participants may draw upon when making sense of effortful experiences and their consequences. Thus, even when particular comparison standards did not inform the initial effort judgment, they may still play an important role in how individuals interpret and cope with its consequences.

This raises the question of how the theoretical framework applies to the observed data. The framework assumes that forming effort judgments requires comparison standards (Morina, 2021; Vlaev et al., 2011), whereas the current design measured standards after the judgment. This order followed from the day-reconstruction procedure, in which an episode is first recalled and rated before the comparison standards relevant to it can be assessed.

Establishing the assumed causal direction would require supplying standards experimentally and measuring effort judgments afterwards (e.g., Meyer et al., 2026), ideally under controlled task demand, which is difficult for effort occurring outside the laboratory. The present data therefore constrain which standards are recruited, or deemed applicable, under a given motivational framing, but they cannot establish that recruitment precedes the judgment.

A second major finding was that, opposite to expectations, upward discrepancy ratings, perceived effort, and prospective effort were higher under allocation than need frustration, while prospective self-efficacy did not differ. One interpretation is that allocation framing made effort salient as active investment in meaningful or goal-relevant activity, consistent with the broader effort paradox: effort can be high not only when an activity feels burdensome, but also when it is construed as investment in something important (Inzlicht et al., 2018). At the same time, this effect should not be interpreted independently of activity selection. Participants selected the two highest-effort episodes separately in each session, and the characteristics of the focal episodes may not be identical across framings. Allocation framing may have highlighted activities involving greater goal investment, persistence, or task demands, which could partly explain higher perceived and prospective effort under allocation.

Although task complexity did not differ significantly between framings, such a selection effect cannot be ruled out. Exploratory moderation analyses further qualified this pattern: depressive symptoms and anhedonia were more positively associated with perceived effort under need-frustration framing, with no comparable moderation for perceived task complexity.

Thus, need-frustration framing may be particularly sensitive to symptom-related effort burden, whereas allocation framing may make effort salient as a goal-driven investment.

Moreover, because the manipulation redefined what "effort" denoted, cross-framing differences in effort ratings reflect differences in construal rather than in sensed exertion.

Under allocation framing, a high rating signals voluntary investment, which carries little motivational cost to report; under need-frustration framing, it signals burden. Higher perceived and prospective effort under allocation therefore need not indicate that activities felt harder. The same applies to the larger upward discrepancies under allocation, which may reflect a gap in voluntary investment rather than a shortfall. Endorsement effects are less affected by this issue, because they concern which standards apply within a framing rather than the level of the ratings.

The clearest instruction effect emerged for upward criteria-based comparisons. Needfrustration framing increased endorsement of standards such as "this should feel less effortful," and the exploratory relevance score that combined endorsement and intensity supported this pattern. This pattern fits the idea that construing effort in terms of needfrustration orients people toward unmet demands, affective burden, and violated expectations (Grund et al., 2024; Koriat et al., 2014). In depression, such criteria-based standards may be especially consequential, e.g., when everyday effort is appraised as evidence that one is failing to function "normally" or falling short of internalized expectations, consistent with prior work on aversive and self-critical comparisons in psychopathology (Meyer et al., 2024; Morina et al., 2022). Clinically, these standards may be promising targets because they identify not only that an activity feels effortful, but the implicit "should" against which that effort becomes problematic. This is consistent with cognitive approaches of depression that identify rigid "should" statements as maladaptive cognitions contributing to depressive symptoms (Ellis, 1997). However, while our data are consistent with the possibility that upward criteria-based comparisons are perceived as particularly threatening, we did not directly assess the motivational meaning of these comparisons. Future work should measure these appraisals directly rather than infer motivational meaning from standard type or direction.

A related interpretation of more frequent "should" standards under need-frustration than under allocation is that the two framings shift the desired level of effort rather than effort construal per se. That is, effort-by-need-frustration implies that the desired level of effort is as low as possible, whereas effort-by-allocation allows a higher desired level of effort.

Criteriabased standards represent desired states more directly than social, temporal, or counterfactual standards, which describe what others experience, what one has personally experienced, or what might have been.

A framing that sets the desired level of effort to a minimum may therefore make "should" standards specifically applicable, rather than making effort feel worse in general. This account may also explain why the criteria-based effect was the only one that held on the stricter relevance criterion. This suggests that future research should consider assessing desired effort in addition to perceived effort.

The opposite pattern for upward temporal-past comparisons was contrary to our directional expectation. We expected lower-effort past standards to be more salient under need frustration, because they can highlight a discrepancy between current and previous functioning. Instead, allocation framing increased endorsement of such standards. Note that this effect was not robust and should not be interpreted substantively: it did not survive correction for multiple comparisons or the validity sensitivity analysis, showed differential carryover, and was absent in first-session data. The following interpretation is therefore tentative. If anything, allocation framing may have recruited lower-effort past episodes as reminders of prior capability ("I have done this with less effort before"), rather than as a signal of loss over time. Whether upward temporal comparisons threaten or support motivation likely depends on their appraisal and content rather than their direction alone.

Need-frustration framing also increased endorsement of downward counterfactual comparison standards, a finding that was not predicted by the initial directional account. This effect should be interpreted cautiously because standards were assessed after effort ratings had been made, and effects on the corresponding relevance-score contrast did not survive correction. Downward counterfactual comparisons may therefore constitute tertiary appraisals of an experience already appraised as negative, rather than comparisons that shaped the initial effort rating. Tertiary comparisons have been conceptualized as potentially providing short-term relief from negative affect or serving a self-protective motive, while remaining embedded in a comparison-focused construal (Morina, 2021). For example, after construing an activity as burdensome and feeling bad as a result, participants may have considered how it could have been even more effortful under worse conditions. Thus, the finding is best taken as evidence that need-frustration framing changes the kinds of counterfactual standards participants can generate or endorse, not as evidence that downward counterfactuals necessarily functioned as motivationally benign standards during the initial effort judgment.

Our findings have methodological and clinical implications. Methodologically, they show that the comparison standards people associate with everyday effort judgments can be elicited in concrete, idiosyncratic form. Future research may extend this approach and examine how direct manipulation of reference frames influences effort judgments and their downstream behavioral consequences, including activity initiation, persistence, and disengagement. Clinically, this approach may help identify modifiable targets for behavioral activation and related interventions. A depressed individual who experiences showering, cooking, or studying as highly effortful may not only be reporting high effort, but comparing the activity with a rigid "should", a better-functioning past self, or an imagined worse alternative. These standards imply different intervention questions: Is the standard self-critical or realistic? Is the past-self comparison experienced as loss or capability? Does the counterfactual provide relief, resignation, or renewed engagement? Such questions may help tailor interventions to the reference frame that gives effort its motivational meaning. This approach may be particularly well suited to cognitive behavioral therapy, as it aligns with cognitive restructuring techniques that examine the evidential basis of rigid should statements and promote more flexible appraisals of effort. It may also complement behavioral activation by helping patients reconsider effort-related beliefs that impede the initiation of and sustained engagement in meaningful or rewarding activities.

Limitations

Several limitations qualify these conclusions. First, the sample consisted of individuals with mild to moderately severe depressive symptoms and was predominantly highly educated, limiting generalizability to clinically diagnosed or more severe depression. Second, all effort judgments were retrospective and concerned activities recalled from the previous day; depression-related negative recall tendencies may have elevated overall ratings, although they are unlikely to explain within-person differences between framings. Third, comparison standards were assessed explicitly and after participants provided effort ratings; the design therefore cannot determine whether the reported standards were spontaneously active during the initial effort judgment or reconstructed during the subsequent assessment.

Fourth, comparison intensity and discrepancy were assessed only for endorsed standards, limiting interpretation to the generated comparisons. Fifth, the manipulation changed the definition of effort itself. This was theoretically intended, but it means that differences in effort ratings reflect shifts in semantic construal and should not be equated with changes in effort perception in a narrower psychophysical sense. Moreover, it is possible that the allocation framing was less fully adopted than the need-frustration framing. The less reliable dominant category codes from the manipulation check provide some support for this interpretation: 72.5% of explanations given under need-frustration framing were dominated by needfrustration content, whereas 55.9% of explanations given under allocation framing were dominated by allocation content, with 23.5% still dominated by need-frustration content. For individuals with depressive symptoms, construing effort as voluntary investment may be less intuitive than construing it as burden. Effects attributed to allocation framing, including the temporal-past effect, should therefore be interpreted with this asymmetry in mind. Future studies should assess spontaneous construal of effort and examine whether they predict the standards people recruit. Sixth, the present study concerns judged effort rather than independently measured exertion, and activities, effort, and complexity were all self-reported.

Endorsement was binary and could not capture partial applicability. Notably, framing effects emerged for endorsement, which asked whether a standard applied, but not for intensity, which asked how strongly it had been considered. Seventh, without a non-dysphoric comparison group, it remains to be clarified whether the observed effects generalize beyond individuals with depressive symptoms. Future work should combine structured comparison assessments with experimental standard manipulations, ecological momentary assessment, behavioral effort tasks, and direct measures of threat, challenge, attainability, and commitment appraisals. Finally, the a priori power analysis targeted the two-way interaction under an optimistic crossover assumption; simple-effect contrasts had considerably lower simulated power (≈ 53%), and power for three-way interactions and binary outcomes was not estimated. Non-significant contrasts should therefore not be interpreted as evidence of absent effects.

Conclusions

In conclusion, our study suggests that motivational framing can alter the comparison standards people with depressive symptoms identify as relevant to everyday effort. The Supplemental Material Supplemental Appendix A: Table S1. Exploratory prototypes of focal comparison standards

Temporal-past "Have there been previous occasions when you associated the same task or activity with less effort?" Criteria-based "Do you think that you actually should associate the same task or activity with lower effort?" "Do you think that you actually should associate the same task or activity with more effort?" Counterfactual "Do you think that the same task or activity would have been associated with less effort if certain things in the past had occurred differently?" "Do you think that the same task or activity would have been associated with more effort if certain things in the past had occurred differently?" Verbatim German effort framing instructions Effort by allocation: Mit "Anstrengung" meinen wir, wie viel Sie von sich aus in eine Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit investieren wollen. Hierbei geht es also um den Aufwand, den Sie aufbringen wollen, um eine Tätigkeit auszuführen oder ein bestimmtes Ziel zu erreichen. Aufgaben und Tätigkeiten, für die wir uns anstrengen, sind uns häufig besonders wichtig und bedeutsam, und helfen uns dabei, wichtige Ziele zu erreichen. Bei der folgenden Frage geht es darum, wie sehr Sie sich bei der Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit während der jeweiligen Episode angestrengt haben. Dies geht häufig damit einher, wie wichtig und bedeutsam die Tätigkeit für Sie und für die Erreichung Ihrer Ziele war. Effort by need-frustration: "Mit "Anstrengung" meinen wir, wie viel eine Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit Ihnen emotional abverlangt. Hierbei geht es also um die emotionalen Anforderungen, die eine Tätigkeit an Sie stellt, die häufig mit negativen Gefühlen einhergehen. Aufgaben und Tätigkeiten, die für uns anstrengend sind, werden z.B. häufig als belastend, hinderlich, ermüdend, oder frustrierend empfunden. Bei der folgenden Frage geht es darum, welche emotionalen Anforderungen die Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit während der jeweiligen Episode an Sie gestellt hat. Dies geht häufig damit einher, wie belastend, hinderlich, ermüdend, oder frustrierend die Tätigkeit für Sie war." 10 Unmöglich große Anstrengung 9 Maximale Anstrengung 8 Extreme Anstrengung 7 Sehr große Anstrengung 6 Große Anstrengung 5 Mäßige Anstrengung 4 Geringe Anstrengung 3 Sehr geringe Anstrengung 2 Extrem geringe Anstrengung 1 Überhaupt keine Anstrengung Verbatim German instructions for comparison-standard prompts Social upward instructions: 0) "Sie haben angegeben, dass Sie mit der Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit [Aktivität] während Episode [Nr.] eine [Anstrengungswert] verbinden." ; 1) "Gibt es Personen in Ihrem Umfeld, von denen Sie glauben, dass sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eine niedrigere Anstrengung verbinden würden?" ; 2) "Sie haben angegeben, dass es in Ihrem Umfeld Personen gibt, von denen Sie glauben, dass sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eine niedrigere Anstrengung verbinden würden. Bitte beschreiben Sie kurz, in welchem Verhältnis Sie zu dieser/n Person(en) stehen. Falls Ihnen viele Personen einfallen, dann reicht ein Beispiel aus." ; 3) "Als Sie vorhin Ihr gestriges Anstrengungserleben eingeschätzt haben, wie stark haben Sie dabei an diese Person(en) gedacht? (1 sehr wenig -7 sehr stark)"; 4) "Im Vergleich zu dieser/n Person(en) war mein eigenes Anstrengungserleben gestern: (1 genau gleich -7 sehr viel größer)". Temporal upward instructions: 1) "Gab es schon mal Zeiten, in denen Sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eine niedrigere Anstrengung verbunden haben?" ; 2) "Sie haben angegeben, dass es schon mal Zeiten gab, in denen Sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eine niedrigere Anstrengung verbunden haben. Bitte beschreiben Sie kurz, zu welchem Zeitpunkt dies (beispielsweise) der Fall war." ; 3) "Als Sie vorhin Ihr gestriges Anstrengungserleben eingeschätzt haben, wie stark haben Sie dabei an diese Zeitpunkte in der Vergangenheit gedacht?" ; 4) "Im Vergleich zu diesen Zeitpunkten in der Vergangenheit war mein eigenes Anstrengungserleben gestern: (1 genau gleich -7 sehr viel größer)" Criteria-based upward instructions: 1) "Haben Sie das Gefühl, dass Sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eigentlich eine niedrigere Anstrengung verbinden sollten?" ; 2) "Sie haben angegeben, dass Sie das Gefühl haben, dass Sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eigentlich eine niedrigere Anstrengung verbinden sollten. Bitte beschreiben Sie kurz Ihre Kriterien, Maßstäbe, oder Erwartungen daran, welches Anstrengungserleben mit dieser Tätigkeit einhergehen sollte." ; 3) "Als Sie vorhin Ihr gestriges Anstrengungserleben eingeschätzt haben, wie stark haben Sie dabei an diese Kriterien, Maßstäbe, oder Erwartungen gedacht?" ; 4) "Im Vergleich zu diesen Kriterien, Maßstäben, oder Erwartungen war mein eigenes Anstrengungserleben gestern: (1 genau gleich -7 sehr viel größer)" Counterfactual upward instructions: 1) "Glauben Sie, dass die gleiche Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit mit einer niedrigeren Anstrengung verbunden gewesen wäre, wenn in der Vergangenheit bestimmte Dinge anders gelaufen wären?" ; 2) "Sie haben angegeben, dass Sie glauben, dass die gleiche Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit mit einer niedrigeren Anstrengung verbunden gewesen wäre, wenn in der Vergangenheit bestimmte Dinge anders gelaufen wären. Bitte beschreiben Sie kurz, welche Dinge in der Vergangenheit hätten anders laufen müssen." ; 3) "Als Sie vorhin Ihr gestriges Anstrengungserleben eingeschätzt haben, wie stark haben Sie dabei an diese Dinge gedacht, die hätten anders laufen müssen?" ; 4) "Im Vergleich zu der Vorstellung, dass diese Dinge in der Vergangenheit anders gelaufen wären, war mein eigenes Anstrengungserleben gestern: (1 genau gleich -7 sehr viel größer)". Social downward instructions: 1) "Gibt es Personen in Ihrem Umfeld, von denen Sie glauben, dass sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eine höhere Anstrengung verbinden würden?" ; 2) "Sie haben angegeben, dass es in Ihrem Umfeld Personen gibt, von denen Sie glauben, dass sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eine höhere Anstrengung verbinden würden. Bitte beschreiben Sie kurz, in welchem Verhältnis Sie zu dieser/n Person(en) stehen. Falls Ihnen viele Personen einfallen, dann reicht ein Beispiel aus." ; 3) "Als Sie vorhin Ihr gestriges Anstrengungserleben eingeschätzt haben, wie stark haben Sie dabei an diese Person(en) gedacht? (1 sehr wenig -7 sehr stark)"; 4) "Im Vergleich zu dieser/n Person(en) war mein eigenes Anstrengungserleben gestern: (1 genau gleich -7 sehr viel kleiner)". Temporal downward instructions: 1) "Gab es schon mal Zeiten, in denen Sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eine höhere Anstrengung verbunden haben?" ; 2) "Sie haben angegeben, dass es schon mal Zeiten gab, in denen Sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eine höhere Anstrengung verbunden haben. Bitte beschreiben Sie kurz, zu welchem Zeitpunkt dies (beispielsweise) der Fall war." ; 3) "Als Sie vorhin Ihr gestriges Anstrengungserleben eingeschätzt haben, wie stark haben Sie dabei an diese Zeitpunkte in der Vergangenheit gedacht?" ; 4) "Im Vergleich zu diesen Zeitpunkten in der Vergangenheit war mein eigenes Anstrengungserleben gestern: (1 genau gleich -7 sehr viel kleiner)" AI-assisted manipulation / content checks Manipulation check.

We conducted an exploratory AI-assisted manipulation/content check of the open-text explanations participants provided after rating effort. Coding was performed row-wise using a locally hosted large language model (gpt-oss-120b). The model was blind to instruction condition; condition labels were merged back only after coding. Each response was rated from 0 to 3 for need-frustration content and allocation content. Needfrustration content reflected burden, fatigue, frustration, pressure, aversion, emotional demand, overwhelm, or self-critical struggle. Allocation content reflected deliberate investment, goal pursuit, persistence, task importance, usefulness, meaning, or value. The model also assigned a dominant attribution category: need frustration, allocation, mixed, neither, or unclear.

Standard validity check.

For the standard-validity check, the input consisted of all endorsed open-text comparison-standard descriptions. The model coded whether each description showed an obvious mismatch with the intended comparison-standard type or comparison direction. The model was instructed to code leniently: brief or elliptical responses were retained unless they clearly contradicted the intended prompt. Responses were coded for standard-type validity, direction validity, and whether they should be recoded as nonendorsed in sensitivity analyses because of an obvious mismatch.

Prototype standards. Finally, we used AI-assisted prototype extraction to characterize the content of the three focal comparison cells that differed by instruction: upward criteriabased, upward temporal-past, and downward counterfactual standards. Obvious mismatches identified in the validity check were excluded before prototype extraction. The model was instructed to identify recurring, clinically interpretable prototypes separately by instruction condition, using the participant's standard description as the primary source and the activity and open effort explanation as context. Outputs were reviewed for plausibility and edited for concision.

Human-LLM agreement. To evaluate the AI-assisted coding, two authors independently coded random subsamples of the open-text data, blind to instruction condition and to the LLM codes. The subsamples comprised 40 open-text effort explanations (20 per condition) and 80 standard descriptions. For the latter, LLM-flagged mismatches were oversampled (25 of 80) so that enough mismatches were available for comparison. Agreement between the two human raters served as the benchmark for LLM-human agreement.

For need-frustration content (scored 0-3), the LLM agreed with the human raters as well as the raters agreed with each other (quadratically weighted κ = .65-.69 vs. .67), and most ratings were within one scale point (LMM-human: both 92.5; human-human: 95.0%). For allocation content (0-3), LLM-human agreement was somewhat lower than human-human agreement (κw = .61-.67 vs. .83; within one point: 80.0-87.5% vs. 95.0%). Agreement on the dominant attribution category was lower for all pairs, and lowest for the LLM (κ = .29-.37 vs.

.49); dominant categories should therefore be interpreted with caution.

Most importantly, the two human raters' codes reproduced the critical manipulationcheck pattern: Need-frustration content was higher under need-frustration framing (rater A: M = 1.80 vs. 0.75; rater B: 1.90 vs. 0.95; LLM: 1.65 vs. 0.75), and allocation content was higher under allocation framing (rater A: 0.45 vs. 2.05; rater B: 0.85 vs. 2.25; LLM: 0.45 vs. 1.55; Welch |t|s ≥ 3.06, ps ≤ .004).

Agreement on obvious mismatches between intended standard type / direction and the participants' open description of the standard was low but similar for all pairs (κ = .35-.36; raw agreement 76-86%). Judging mismatches in brief descriptions therefore appears inherently difficult rather than a specific weakness of the LLM. The human raters also flagged fewer mismatches (15.0% and 8.8%) than the LLM (31.3%, fixed by the oversampling). Because the sensitivity analysis recoded all LLM-flagged mismatches as non-endorsed, it was a strict rather than lenient test.

Ethics statement

Exploratory analyses of standard relevance scores (endorsement x Intensity)

Since comparison intensity ratings are conditional on comparison endorsement and thus reflect only a selective subset of the data, we ran the following exploratory analysis. To integrate endorsement and intensity, we computed an exploratory relevance score in which non-endorsed standards were coded as 0 and endorsed standards were coded with their reported intensity. A mixed-effects model revealed a significant Instruction Type × Direction × Standard interaction, χ²(3) = 17.47, p < .001. Follow-up contrasts showed that this interaction was primarily driven by upward criteria-based comparisons, which were more relevant in the Need Frustration condition (M = 2.60, SE = 0.19) than in the Allocation condition (M = 1.70, SE = 0.19), difference = 0.90, p < .001, BH-adjusted p = .001. No other Instruction Type contrasts survived correction. The estimates are shown in Figure S3.

Supplemental Appendix E: Deviations from and additions to the preregistration.

(1) The a priori power analysis was conducted but not included in the preregistration.

(2) Direction-specific follow-up models and pairwise contrasts were not preregistered; they were conducted to decompose the significant three-way interaction, and pairwise contrasts were additionally BH-corrected.

(3) Linear mixed models were estimated with REML, which was not specified.

(4) The preregistration stated that sensitivity analyses would not exclude data; the validity sensitivity analysis recoded 58 descriptions flagged as obvious mismatches as non-endorsed, and results are reported alongside the full-data analyses.

(5) For one endorsement explicitly identified by the participant as a misclick (which we recoded as not endorsed), residual follow-up ratings were set to missing.

(6) Order/session analyses, the relevance score, moderation analyses, AI-assisted content coding, and the likelihood-ratio test were not preregistered and are reported as exploratory.

Kroenke et al., 2009; see Measures). Exclusion criteria were elevated alcohol use (≥ 5 occasions per week on average), elevated use of other drugs (≥ 3 occasions per week on average), or a diagnosed condition likely to impair cognitive abilities, such as dementia or another neurodegenerative disease. Participants received 20€ or partial course credits (available for students only) for completion of all parts of the study.

. The primary analyses tested whether interactions between Instruction Type, Comparison Direction, and Comparison Standard Type predicted comparison endorsement and comparison intensity. Initial models included Instruction Type, Comparison Direction, Comparison Standard Type, and their interactions as fixed effects. Episode was included as an additional fixed effect to account for the data structure but was not a focus of hypothesis testing. Participant ID was included as a random factor in all mixed-effects models. The comparison-standard data comprised 1,632 observations (51 participants × 32 judgments). Each Instruction Type × Comparison Direction × Comparison Standard Type cell contained 102 observations.

×

Comparison Direction effect was not supported. Instead, framing effects depended on comparison-standard type (H3): need frustration increased upward criteria-based and downward counterfactual endorsement, whereas allocation increased upward temporal-past endorsement, although the latter effect was not robust (also see Order Effects in Exploratory Analyses, below). Figure 1 displays model-estimated endorsement probabilities.

Figure 1.

Figure 1. Model-estimated endorsement probabilities for comparison standards by instruction

Figure 2.

Figure 2. Exploratory moderation of perceived effort by depressive symptoms/anhedonia.

results do not support a simple upward-versus-downward account. Instead, need frustration increased upward criteria-based and downward counterfactual standards. By combining an experimental manipulation of effort construal with a structured assessment of idiosyncratic comparison standards, the study extends work on the effort paradox by showing that motivational framing changes what everyday effort is compared against, which may in turn influence whether effort feels costly or valuable. These comparison standards may provide clinically useful targets for reshaping maladaptive effort-related cognition. Future work must determine which appraisals and comparison contents promote persistence, re-engagement, and well-being in daily life.

Criteria-based downward instructions: 1) "Haben Sie das Gefühl, dass Sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eigentlich eine höhere Anstrengung verbinden sollten?";

"Sie haben angegeben, dass Sie das Gefühl haben, dass Sie mit der gleichen Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit eigentlich eine höhere Anstrengung verbinden sollten. Bitte beschreiben Sie kurz Ihre Kriterien, Maßstäbe, oder Erwartungen daran, welches Anstrengungserleben mit dieser Tätigkeit einhergehen sollte.";

"Als Sie vorhin Ihr gestriges Anstrengungserleben eingeschätzt haben, wie stark haben Sie dabei an diese Kriterien, Maßstäbe, oder Erwartungen gedacht?";

"Im Vergleich zu diesen Kriterien, Maßstäben, oder Erwartungen war mein eigenes Anstrengungserleben gestern: (1 genau gleich -7 sehr viel kleiner)" Counterfactual downward instructions: 1) "Glauben Sie, dass die gleiche Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit mit einer höheren Anstrengung verbunden gewesen wäre, wenn in der Vergangenheit bestimmte Dinge anders gelaufen wären?";

"Sie haben angegeben, dass Sie glauben, dass die gleiche Aufgabe oder Tätigkeit mit einer höheren Anstrengung verbunden gewesen wäre, wenn in der Vergangenheit bestimmte Dinge anders gelaufen wären. Bitte beschreiben Sie kurz, welche Dinge in der Vergangenheit hätten anders laufen müssen.";

"Als Sie vorhin Ihr gestriges Anstrengungserleben eingeschätzt haben, wie stark haben Sie dabei an diese Dinge gedacht, die hätten anders laufen müssen?";

"Im Vergleich zu der Vorstellung, dass diese Dinge in der Vergangenheit anders gelaufen wären, war mein eigenes Anstrengungserleben gestern: (1 genau gleich -7 sehr viel kleiner)".

If there are things we can improve, please email us at metacheck@scienceverse.org or leave an issue at github.com/scienceverse/metacheck/issues.
